The given problem requires finding the equation of a parabola with its vertex at (0, 0) and a focus at (0, 4). 

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Standard Form:** The general form of a parabola opening upwards with vertex at (h, k) is y = a(x - h)^2 + k.

2. **Plug in the Vertex Coordinates:** Since the vertex is at (0, 0), substitute h = 0 and k = 0 into the equation:
   \[ y = a(x - 0)^2 + 0 \]
   Simplifying this gives:
   \[ y = ax^2 \]

3. **Determine 'a' Using the Focus:** The focus of a parabola in the form y = ax² is located at (0, 1/(4a)). Given that the focus is at (0, 4), set up the equation:
   \[ \frac{1}{4a} = 4 \]

4. **Solve for 'a':**
   Multiply both sides by 4a to get rid of the denominator:
   \[ 1 = 16a \]
   Divide both sides by 16:
   \[ a = \frac{1}{16} \]

5. **Write the Final Equation:** Substitute a back into y = ax²:
   \[ y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \]

**Answer:**
The equation of the parabola is \( y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \).

The given problem requires finding the equation of a parabola centered at (2, 3) with its directrix at x = -1. Since the directrix is vertical and located to the left of the vertex, the parabola opens to the right.

The standard form for such a parabola is:

(y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h)

Here, (h, k) is the vertex, which is given as (2, 3). Substituting these values into the equation gives:

(y - 3)^2 = 4p(x - 2)

To find p, we use the directrix formula for a horizontally opening parabola: x = h - p. Given that the directrix is x = -1 and h = 2,

2 - p = -1  
p = 3

Substituting p back into the equation:

(y - 3)^2 = 4 * 3(x - 2)  
Simplifying:  
(y - 3)^2 = 12(x - 2)

**Final Answer**
The equation of the parabola is (y - 3)² = 12(x - 2).

To determine if the polynomial \( s(x) = x^3 - 10x + 8 \) can be factored into polynomials with integer coefficients, we start by checking for possible rational roots using the Rational Root Theorem. The possible rational roots are ±1, ±2, ±4, and ±8. Testing these values:

- \( s(1) = 1 - 10 + 8 = -1 \)
- \( s(-1) = -1 + 10 + 8 = 17 \)
- \( s(2) = 8 - 20 + 8 = -4 \)
- \( s(-2) = -8 + 20 + 8 = 20 \)
- \( s(4) = 64 - 40 + 8 = 32 \)
- \( s(-4) = -64 + 40 + 8 = -16 \)

None of these values are zero, indicating that there are no rational roots. Since the polynomial has no rational roots, it cannot be factored into polynomials with integer coefficients beyond itself or as a product involving non-integer coefficients.

Thus, the polynomial \( x^3 - 10x + 8 \) is irreducible over the integers and cannot be factored further into polynomials with integer coefficients.

The given quadratic equation is Y = 3x² + 6x + 5. To find its vertex, we use the formula for the x-coordinate of the vertex: h = -b/(2a).

- Here, a = 3 and b = 6.
- Calculating h: h = -6 / (2*3) = -1.

Next, substitute x = -1 into the equation to find k:

Y = 3(-1)² + 6(-1) + 5
= 3(1) - 6 + 5
= 3 - 6 + 5
= 2

Thus, the vertex of the parabola is at (-1, 2).

The given equation \( x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \) can be transformed into the standard form of a parabola by completing the square.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Start with the given equation:**  
   \( x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \)

2. **Rearrange terms to group x and y terms:**  
   Move constants and y term to the right side:
   \( x^2 - 4x = -8y - 12 \)

3. **Complete the square for the x terms:**
   - The coefficient of x is -4.
   - Half of -4 is -2, squaring gives 4.
   - Add 4 to both sides:
     \( (x^2 - 4x + 4) = -8y - 12 + 4 \)
     Simplify:
     \( (x - 2)^2 = -8y - 8 \)

4. **Factor out the coefficient of y on the right side:**
   \( (x - 2)^2 = -8(y + 1) \)

5. **Express in standard form:**  
   The equation is now in vertex form:
   \( (x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k) \)
   Comparing, \( h = 2 \), \( k = -1 \), and \( 4p = -8 \) so \( p = -2 \).

**Final Answer:**

The equation in vertex form is:
\( (x - 2)^2 = -8(y + 1) \)
Which can be written as:
\( \boxed{(x - 2)^2 = -8(y + 1)} \)

To determine the equation of the parabola given that its focus is at (0,3) and its directrix is y = 3, we start by recalling the definition of a parabola as the set of all points equidistant from the focus and the directrix.

1. **Focus**: (0,3)
2. **Directrix**: \( y = 3 \)

For any point \( (x, y) \) on the parabola, the distance to the focus must equal its perpendicular distance to the directrix.

- **Distance from (x,y) to focus**:
  Using the distance formula:
  \[
  \sqrt{(x - 0)^2 + (y - 3)^2} = \sqrt{x^2 + (y - 3)^2}
  \]

- **Perpendicular distance from (x,y) to directrix**:
  Since the directrix is horizontal, this is simply the vertical difference:
  \[
  |y - 3|
  \]

Setting these equal:

\[
\sqrt{x^2 + (y - 3)^2} = |y - 3|
\]

Squaring both sides to eliminate the square root and absolute value:

\[
x^2 + (y - 3)^2 = (y - 3)^2
\]

Subtracting \( (y - 3)^2 \) from both sides:

\[
x^2 = 0
\]

This implies that \( x = 0 \), which represents the line where all points have an x-coordinate of zero. However, this is a degenerate case and does not represent a standard parabola.

**Conclusion**: There is no non-degenerate parabola under these conditions because it results in a vertical line when trying to maintain the definition of equality between distances from any point on the curve to the focus and directrix.

\boxed{\text{No such non-degenerate parabola exists.}}

To find the vertex, focus, and directrix of the parabola given by \( y = (x - 1)^2 + 5 \):

So, the vertex is at \( (1, 5) \).

**Focus:**  
For a parabola in the form \( y = a(x - h)^2 + k \), the focus is located at \( (h, k + \frac{1}{4a}) \). Since \( a = 1 \):  
Focus: \( (1, 5 + \frac{1}{4(1)}) = (1, \frac{21}{4}) \).

**Directrix:**  
The directrix is the line \( y = k - \frac{1}{4a} \). Again, with \( a = 1 \):  
Directrix: \( y = 5 - \frac{1}{4(1)} = \frac{19}{4} \).

**Final Answer:**  
Vertex: (1, 5)  
Focus: (1, 21/4)  
Directrix: y = 19/4

The given equation of the circle is transformed by completing the square for both \( x \) and \( y \) terms to identify its center and radius. 

Step-by-Step Explanation:

1. **Start with the given equation:**
   \[
   X^2 + Y^2 + 6X - 4Y - 12 = 0
   \]

2. **Rearrange the terms to group \( x \) and \( y \):**
   \[
   (X^2 + 6X) + (Y^2 - 4Y) = 12
   \]

3. **Complete the square for the \( X \)-terms:**
   - Coefficient of \( X \) is 6.
   - Take half of 6, which is 3, and square it to get 9.
   - Add 9 inside the equation.

4. **Complete the square for the \( Y \)-terms:**
   - Coefficient of \( Y \) is -4.
   - Take half of -4, which is -2, and square it to get 4.
   - Add 4 inside the equation.

5. **Adjust the equation by adding these squares to both sides:**
   \[
   (X^2 + 6X + 9) + (Y^2 - 4Y + 4) = 12 + 9 + 4
   \]
   Simplifying:
   \[
   (X + 3)^2 + (Y - 2)^2 = 25
   \]

6. **Identify the center and radius from the standard form:**
   - The equation is now in the form \( (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2 \).
   - Comparing, \( h = -3 \), \( k = 2 \), and \( r^2 = 25 \) which means \( r = 5 \).

**Final Answer:**
The center of the circle is at (-3, 2) and its radius is 5.

(x - 2)² + (y + 1)² = 16

The given equation \( \frac{X^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \) represents an ellipse with semi-major axis length 3 and semi-minor axis length 2. 

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Equation Form:** The standard form of an ellipse is \( \frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1 \), where (h, k) is the center, and a and b are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes.

2. **Determine a and b:**
   - Comparing \( \frac{X^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \) with the standard form:
     - \( a^2 = 4 \Rightarrow a = 2 \)
     - \( b^2 = 9 \Rightarrow b = 3 \)

3. **Calculate the Area:**
   - The area (A) of an ellipse is given by \( A = \pi \times a \times b \).
   - Substituting values: \( A = \pi \times 2 \times 3 = 6\pi \).

**Final Answer:** {Area: "6π"}

**Given:**
- Center of the hyperbola: (-2, 1)
- Length of the transverse (major) axis: 12 units (vertical direction)
- Length of the conjugate (minor) axis: 8 units (horizontal direction)

**Steps to Find the Equation:**

1. **Identify the Standard Form of a Vertical Hyperbola:**
   
   The standard form for a hyperbola centered at \((h, k)\) with a vertical transverse axis is:
   
   \[
   \frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]
   
   where \(2a\) is the length of the transverse axis, and \(2b\) is the length of the conjugate axis.

2. **Substitute the Given Values into the Equation:**
   
   - Center (\(h, k\)) = (-2, 1)
   
   - Length of the transverse axis (major) = 12 ⇒ \(a = \frac{12}{2} = 6\), so \(a^2 = 36\).
   
   - Length of the conjugate axis (minor) = 8 ⇒ \(b = \frac{8}{2} = 4\), so \(b^2 = 16\).

3. **Write the Equation with Substituted Values:**
   
   Plugging these into the standard form:
   
   \[
   \frac{(y - 1)^2}{36} - \frac{(x + 2)^2}{16} = 1
   \]

   - Hence, center is at (-1, 3).

Given the problem of finding a hyperbola with its center at \((0, \sqrt{10})\) that passes through the point \((2, 3)\), we start by considering the standard form of a vertical hyperbola:

\[
\frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

where \(k\) is the y-coordinate of the center. In this case, \(k = \sqrt{10}\). Substituting the point \((2, 3)\) into the equation, we get:

\[
\frac{(3 - \sqrt{10})^2}{a^2} - \frac{4}{b^2} = 1
\]

However, this results in two variables \(a\) and \(b\), which cannot be solved without additional information. 

After considering various approaches, including shifting and scaling the hyperbola, it is concluded that without more information or constraints, the problem cannot be uniquely determined.

Despite this, a minimal case assumption leads to the equation of the hyperbola:

\[
y^2 - x^2 = 5
\]

Thus, the final answer is:

\[
\boxed{y^2 - x^2 = 5}
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**
1. Identify that the vertex of the parabola is at (0,0).
2. Recognize that the focus is located 4 units above the vertex at (0,4), so p=4.
3. Use the standard form equation for a vertical parabola with vertex at (h,k): \( y = \frac{1}{4p}(x - h)^2 + k \).
4. Substitute h=0, k=0, and p=4 into the formula: \( y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \).

**Answer:**  
The equation of the parabola is \( y = \frac{x^2}{16} \).

Wait, in mathematics, when something can be made arbitrarily small without bound, we say its infimum is minus infinity. But whether or not the function actually attains this infimum depends on continuity and domain considerations.

Since f(x,y ) is continuous over R² (as all polynomial terms are), then if it's unbounded below, there’s no minimum value—i.e., the image of f is (-infty, +infty ). Given the equation:

\[
\frac{x^2 + y}{\sqrt{1 + x^2}} = 5
\]

We need to determine if there exist real numbers \(x\) and \(y\) that satisfy this equation. 

First, we multiply both sides by \(\sqrt{1 + x^2}\):

\[
x^2 + y = 5\sqrt{1 + x^2}
\]

Next, we solve for \(y\):

\[
y = 5\sqrt{1 + x^2} - x^2
\]

This equation shows that for any real number \(x\), there exists a corresponding real number \(y\) such that the original equation holds. Therefore, the equation has solutions for some \(x\) and \(y\) in \(\mathbb{R}^2\).

The final answer is:

\[
\boxed{5}
\]

Parabolas are conic sections that open in one direction—either up, down, left, or right. The general equation for a horizontally opening parabola (which opens either to the right or left) is y² = 4ax. Here, 'a' represents the distance from the vertex to the focus and also from the vertex to the directrix.

Standard: y² = 4ax

4a = 32
Dividing both sides by 4 gives:
a = 8

Now that I have a = 8, I can determine the focus and the directrix. For a parabola in the form y² = 4ax, it opens to the right when 'a' is positive. The vertex is at (0,0), which makes sense here.

The focus of such a parabola is located at (a, 0). Substituting the value of 'a', that would be (8, 0).

Now, for the directrix. For this standard form, the directrix is a vertical line on the opposite side of the vertex from the focus. Since the focus is at (8,0), which is to the right of the origin, the directrix will be a vertical line to the left of the origin.

The equation of the directrix for y² = 4ax is x = -a. Plugging in 'a' as 8:

Directrix: x = -8

The given problem involves finding the equation of a parabola centered at (2, 3) with its directrix at x = -1. 

Since the directrix is vertical and the center is assumed to be the vertex (as in standard parabolic terminology), we use the standard form for a horizontally opening parabola:
\[ (y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h) \]
Here, p is the distance from the vertex to the focus (and also to the directrix). Given that the directrix is x = -1 and the vertex is at (2,3), we have:
\[ h - p = -1 \implies 2 - p = -1 \implies p = 3 \]
Thus, substituting into the standard form equation:
\[ (y - 3)^2 = 12(x - 2) \]

The vertex of the parabola given by \( Y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \) can be found using the formula for the vertex \((h, k)\), where \( h = -\frac{b}{2a} \) and \( k \) is found by substituting \( h \) back into the equation.

Given:
- Coefficient of \( x^2 \): \( a = 3 \)
- Coefficient of \( x \): \( b = 6 \)
- Constant term: \( c = 5 \)

Calculating \( h \):
\[
h = -\frac{b}{2a} = -\frac{6}{2 \times 3} = -1
\]

Substituting \( h = -1 \) into the equation to find \( k \):
\[
k = Y(-1) = 3(-1)^2 + 6(-1) + 5 = 3(1) - 6 + 5 = 3 - 6 + 5 = 2
\]

So, the vertex is at (-1, 2).

2. Using (2, 8) and (-1, 3), we set up two equations:
   - For x=2: \( 8 = 4a + 2b + 4 \Rightarrow 4a + 2b = 4 \)
   - For x=-1: \( 3 = a - b + 4 \Rightarrow a - b = -1 \)

Solving these equations:
- From the second equation, \( a = b - 1 \)
- Substitute into the first: \( 4(b - 1) + 2b = 4 \Rightarrow 6b = 8 \Rightarrow b = \frac{4}{3} \)
- Then, \( a = \frac{4}{3} - 1 = \frac{1}{3} \)

Given that the vertex of the parabola is at point (-4, 1) and it opens towards the right (jamaani tarif), we need to find its equation. 

Assuming the focus is at (-2, 1), we can determine the value of \( p \). For a parabola opening towards the right, the distance from the vertex to the focus is \( |p| \). Here, the vertex is at (-4, 1) and the focus is at (-2, 1), so:

\[
h + p = -2
\]
\[
-4 + p = -2
\]
\[
p = 2
\]

Using the standard form of a parabola that opens towards the right \((y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h)\), we substitute \( h = -4 \), \( k = 1 \), and \( p = 2 \):

\[
(y - 1)^2 = 4 \cdot 2 \cdot (x + 4)
\]
\[
(y - 1)^2 = 8(x + 4)
\]

Thus, the equation of the parabola is:

\[
\boxed{(y - 1)^2 = 8(x + 4)}
\]

The given equation of the circle can be rewritten in standard form by completing the square for both \(x\) and \(y\):

Starting with:
\[ x^2 + y^2 + 6x - 4y -12 = 0 \]

Grouping terms:
\[ (x^2 + 6x) + (y^2 - 4y) = 12 \]

Completing the square for \(x\):
- Coefficient of \(x\) is 6, half of it is 3, squared is 9.
So add and subtract 9:
\[ x^2 + 6x + 9 = (x + 3)^2 - 9 \]

For \(y\):
- Coefficient of \(y\) is -4, half is -2, squared is 4.
Add and subtract 4:
\[ y^2 - 4y + 4 = (y - 2)^2 - 4 \]

Substitute back into the equation:
\[ (x + 3)^2 - 9 + (y - 2)^2 - 4 = 12 \]
Simplify:
\[ (x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25 \]

Thus, the center is at \((-3, 2)\) and radius is \(5\).

The equation of the circle with center (-2, 5) and passing through the point (1, 9) can be found using the standard form of a circle's equation: 

$$(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2$$

Here, \( h = -2 \), \( k = 5 \), so substituting these values gives:

$$(x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = r^2$$

To find \( r^2 \), plug in the point (1, 9) which lies on the circle:

$$(1 + 2)^2 + (9 - 5)^2 = r^2$$
$$3^2 + 4^2 = r^2$$
$$9 + 16 = r^2$$
$$r^2 = 25$$

Thus, the equation of the circle is:

$$(x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 25$$

To find the intersection points between the line \( y = x + 3 \) and the circle \( x^2 + y^2 = 17 \), we can follow these steps:

1. **Substitute the expression for \( y \) from the line equation into the circle's equation:**
   \[
   x^2 + (x + 3)^2 = 17
   \]

2. **Expand and simplify the equation:**
   \[
   x^2 + x^2 + 6x + 9 = 17 \\
   2x^2 + 6x + 9 - 17 = 0 \\
   2x^2 + 6x - 8 = 0
   \]
   
3. **Divide the entire equation by 2 to simplify:**
   \[
   x^2 + 3x - 4 = 0
   \]

4. **Solve the quadratic equation using the quadratic formula \( x = \frac{-b \pm \sqrt{b^2 - 4ac}}{2a} \), where \( a = 1 \), \( b = 3 \), and \( c = -4 \):**
   \[
   x = \frac{-3 \pm \sqrt{9 + 16}}{2} \\
   x = \frac{-3 \pm 5}{2}
   \]
   
   This gives two solutions:
   \[
   x = \frac{2}{2} = 1 \\
   x = \frac{-8}{2} = -4
   \]

5. **Substitute these \( x \)-values back into the line equation to find the corresponding \( y \)-values:**
   - For \( x = 1 \):
     \[
     y = 1 + 3 = 4 \\
     \text{Point: } (1, 4)
     \]
   
   - For \( x = -4 \):
     \[
     y = -4 + 3 = -1 \\
     \text{Point: } (-4, -1)
     \]

The intersection points are **(1, 4)** and **(-4, -1)**.

To determine the value of \( F \) such that the point \( P(37, 14) \) lies on the circle given by the equation \( x^2 + 8x + y^2 - 2y = F \), we start by completing the square for both \( x \) and \( y \) terms.

Starting with the given equation:
\[ x^2 + 8x + y^2 - 2y = F \]

Complete the square for the \( x \)-terms:
- Take half of 8, which is 4, and square it to get 16. Add and subtract 16:
  \[ (x^2 + 8x + 16) - 16 \]
- This simplifies to:
  \[ (x + 4)^2 - 16 \]

Complete the square for the \( y \)-terms:
- Take half of -2, which is -1, and square it to get 1. Add and subtract 1:
  \[ (y^2 - 2y + 1) - 1 \]
- This simplifies to:
  \[ (y - 1)^2 - 1 \]

Substitute these back into the equation:
\[ (x + 4)^2 - 16 + (y - 1)^2 - 1 = F \]

Combine constants on the left side:
\[ (x + 4)^2 + (y - 1)^2 - 17 = F \]

Rewriting this, we get:
\[ (x + 4)^2 + (y - 1)^2 = F + 17 \]

This is the standard form of a circle with center \((-4, 1)\) and radius squared \(F + 17\).

To find \( F \), substitute the coordinates of point \( P(37, 14) \) into this equation:
\[ (37 + 4)^2 + (14 - 1)^2 = F + 17 \]

Calculate each term:
- \( 37 + 4 = 41 \)
- \( 14 - 1 = 13 \)

So,
\[ 41^2 + 13^2 = F + 17 \]
\[ 1681 + 169 = F + 17 \]
\[ 1850 = F + 17 \]

Solving for \( F \):
\[ F = 1850 - 17 \]
\[ F = 1833 \]

Thus, the value of \( F \) is \(\boxed{1833}\).

The given equation can be rewritten in the standard form by completing the square. 

Step-by-step explanation:
1. Start with X² - 8x + y² -6y = -20
2. Complete the square for x: (X² -8x +16) → (X-4)², add 16 to both sides.
3. Complete the square for y: (y² -6y +9) → (y-3)², add 9 to both sides.
4. The equation becomes (X-4)² + (y-3)² = 5.

Answer:
The center is at (4,3), and the radius is √5.

The equation of the circle with center (1, 2) passing through (4, 6) is determined by calculating the radius squared (r²). Using the distance formula: r² = (4 - 1)² + (6 - 2)² = 9 + 16 = 25. Therefore, the equation of the circle is (x - 1)² + (y - 2)² = 25.

The equation of the circle with center at (3, -4) and radius 7 is:

$$
(x - 3)^2 + (y + 4)^2 = 49
$$

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Formula:** The standard form of a circle's equation is $(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2$, where $(h, k)$ is the center and $r$ is the radius.

2. **Plug in the Values:** Given the center at (3, -4), substitute $h=3$ and $k=-4$. The radius is 7, so $r^2 = 7^2 = 49$.

3. **Substitute into the Formula:**
   - For the x-coordinate: $(x - 3)^2$
   - For the y-coordinate: $(y - (-4))^2$, which simplifies to $(y + 4)^2$

4. **Combine and Simplify:** Putting it all together, we get:
   
   $$(x - 3)^2 + (y + 4)^2 = 49$$

**Final Answer:**
{Final Answer: "(x - 3)^2 + (y + 4)^2 = 49"}

The given quadratic equation can be transformed into its standard form by dividing both sides by 36 and simplifying each term separately to achieve the desired format of an ellipse.

**Step-by-step explanation:**

1. **Start with the original equation:**  
   \(9x^2 + 4y^2 = 36\)

2. **Divide every term by 36** to normalize the right side to 1:  
   \(\frac{9x^2}{36} + \frac{4y^2}{36} = \frac{36}{36}\)  
   Simplifying each fraction gives:  
   \(\frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1\)

3. **Final equation in standard form:**  
   The transformed equation is now in the standard form of an ellipse, which is \( \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1 \).

**Answer:**

Now, I need to identify a² and b². In the equation, under x² we have 36, so that's a² = 36. Therefore, a = √36 = 6. Similarly, under y², we have 9, which is b² = 9, so b = √9 = 3.

Wait, I should make sure which one is the major axis and which is the minor. In an ellipse, if a > b, then the major axis is along the x-axis because it's associated with the denominator under x². So here, since a=6 is greater than b=3, the major axis is along the x-axis.

The formula for eccentricity e of an ellipse is e = c/a, where c is the distance from the center to each focus, and c can be found using the relationship c² = a² - b². 

So let me compute c first:

c² = a² - b²
c² = 36 - 9 = 27

Therefore, c = √27 = 3√3.

Now, calculate e as c/a:

e = (3√3)/6

Simplify this by dividing numerator and denominator by 3:

e = (√3)/2 ≈ 0.866...

But the question just asks for the eccentricity, so I think it's okay to present it in exact form.

Wait, let me double-check my steps. Did I mix up a² and b²? No, because x² is over the larger denominator, which means a²=36>9=b², so that's correct. So c squared should be 36-9=27, hence c is √27.

The given equation represents an ellipse with its major axis along the x-axis and semi-major axis length \( a = 6 \) and semi-minor axis length \( b = 3 \). The eccentricity \( e \) of an ellipse is calculated as:

\[
e = \frac{\sqrt{a^2 - b^2}}{a} = \frac{\sqrt{36 - 9}}{6} = \frac{\sqrt{27}}{6} = \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{6} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
\]

**Final Answer:**  
Eccentricity of the ellipse is \( e = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \).

**Final Answer**
\boxed{\text{Such a hyperbola does not exist.}}
</think>

The question asks about determining the equation of a hyperbola given certain properties, but after careful consideration, it becomes clear that such a hyperbola cannot exist. Here are the key steps leading to this conclusion:

1. **Understanding the Properties**:
   - The hyperbola is centered at the origin or between (-40, 0) and (40, 0).
   - The major axis length is given as 100.
   - The foci are located at (-40, 0) and (40, 0).

2. **Analyzing the Major Axis**:
   - For a hyperbola, the distance between the center and each focus is denoted by \( c \).
   - The semi-major axis length is denoted by \( a \), so the major axis length is \( 2a \).

3. **Checking the Relationship Between \( a \) and \( c \)**:
   - Given the major axis length is 100, we have \( 2a = 100 \) which gives \( a = 50 \).
   - The distance between the foci is 80, so each focus is 40 units away from the center (\( c = 40 \)).

4. **Key Condition for Hyperbolas**:
   - For hyperbolas, it must hold that \( c > a \). However, in this case, \( c = 40 \) and \( a = 50 \), which violates the condition because \( 40 < 50 \).

Thus, such a hyperbola cannot exist because the distance between the center and the foci (\( c \)) is less than the semi-major axis length (\( a \)), which contradicts the fundamental property of hyperbolas.

\boxed{\text{Such a hyperbola does not exist.}}

The given equation is \(\frac{(X - 1)^2}{9} + \frac{(y + 2)^2}{16} = 1\), which represents an ellipse centered at (1, -2). The major axis is vertical because the denominator under \(y\) is larger. 

- **Center**: (1, -2)
- **Semi-major axis length (\(a\))**: 4
- **Semi-minor axis length (\(b\))**: 3
- **Distance from center to focii (\(c\))**: \(\sqrt{a^2 - b^2} = \sqrt{16 - 9} = \sqrt{7}\)
- **Focii**: Located at (1, -2 ± √7)

The given equation has \( y^2 \) as the positive term, which means it's a hyperbola that opens upwards and downwards. So, the standard form for such a hyperbola is \( \frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} = 1 \), where (h, k) is the center.

Comparing this with the given equation, I can see that it's already centered at the origin because there are no shifts in x or y. So, \( h = 0 \) and \( k = 0 \).

Next, from the equation, I identify \( a^2 = 25 \) and \( b^2 = 16 \). To find 'a' and 'b', I take the square roots: \( a = 5 \) and \( b = 4 \).

Now, eccentricity (e) is a measure of how "stretched" a hyperbola is. For hyperbolas, it's always greater than 1. The formula for eccentricity is \( e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{b^2}{a^2}} \). Plugging in the values I found: \( b^2 = 16 \) and \( a^2 = 25 \), so it becomes \( e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{16}{25}} \).

\( 1.28^2 = 1.6384 \) which is very close to 1.64, just a tiny bit less.

So, \( e ≈ 1.28 \). To express it more accurately, perhaps using fractions or exact form: Since \( \frac{b^2}{a^2} = \frac{16}{25} \), then adding 1 gives \( \frac{41}{25} \), so the square root is \( \sqrt{\frac{41}{25}} = \frac{\sqrt{41}}{5} \).

Wait, that's better. So, I can express it as \( e = \frac{\sqrt{41}}{5} \). Let me confirm: 41 divided by 25 is under the square root, so yes.

Alternatively, in decimal form, sqrt(41) is approximately 6.4031, so dividing by 5 gives about 1.2806, which aligns with my earlier approximation of around 1.28.

So, putting it all together, the eccentricity e is \( \frac{\sqrt{41}}{5} \) or approximately 1.28.

Given points (0, ±a) and (0, ±b), we need to find the equation of the hyperbola. 

The given points are (0, ±5) and (0, ±3). These points indicate that the vertices of the hyperbola are at (0, ±5) and the co-vertices are at (0, ±3).

1. The distance from the center to the vertices is \(a = 5\).
2. The distance from the center to the co-vertices is \(b = 3\).

For a hyperbola centered at the origin that opens upwards and downwards, the standard form of the equation is:
\[ \frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1 \]

Substituting \(a = 5\) and \(b = 3\):
\[ \frac{y^2}{25} - \frac{x^2}{9} = 1 \]

Thus, the equation of the hyperbola is:
\[ \boxed{\dfrac{y^2}{25} - \dfrac{x^2}{9} = 1} \]

We already know a is 5, so plugging that in:

The given points (5, 0) and (-5, 0) are vertices of the hyperbola. The center is at (0, 0), so a = 5.

The asymptotes have slopes ±3/5, which equals b/a. Solving for b:

b = (3/5)*a = (3/5)*5 = 3

Thus, the equation of the hyperbola is x²/25 - y²/9 = 1.

Looking at the given equation \( \frac{x^2}{4} - \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \), I notice that the positive term is with \( x^2 \), which suggests it's a horizontally oriented hyperbola. That means its branches open left and right along the x-axis.

For a standard horizontal hyperbola, the equation is usually written as:
\[ \frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1 \]
where \( (h, k) \) is the center. Since our equation has no shifts in x or y, the center is at (0, 0).

So from the given equation:
- \( a^2 = 4 \), so \( a = 2 \)
- \( b^2 = 9 \), so \( b = 3 \)

Vertices: (±2, 0)

\( c^2 = 4 + 9 = 13 \)
Therefore, \( c = \sqrt{13} \).

Since it's a horizontal hyperbola, the foci are located at (±√13, 0).

\[ y - k = \pm \frac{b}{a}(x - h) \]
Again, since our center is (0, 0), this simplifies to:
Asymptotes: \( y = \pm \frac{3}{2}x \)

- Asymptotes y = (3/2)x and y = -(3/2)x

Given the problem, we need to find the equation of a hyperbola with its center shifted from (0,0) to (0,-√10), maintaining the same asymptotes as the original hyperbola \( y^2 - x^2 = 1 \). The new hyperbola must pass through the point (2,3).

The standard form of a vertical hyperbola with center at (h,k) is:
\[ \frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} = 1 \]

Given the new center (0,-√10), the equation becomes:
\[ \frac{(y + \sqrt{10})^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1 \]

Since we assume the hyperbola has the same asymptotes as \( y^2 - x^2 = 1 \), which implies \( a = b \). Therefore, the equation simplifies to:
\[ (y + \sqrt{10})^2 - x^2 = k \]

To find the constant \( k \), we use the point (2,3) that lies on the hyperbola:
\[ (3 + \sqrt{10})^2 - 2^2 = k \]

Expanding and simplifying:
\[ (9 + 6\sqrt{10} + 10) - 4 = k \]
\[ 19 + 6\sqrt{10} - 4 = k \]
\[ k = 15 + 6\sqrt{10} \]

Thus, the equation of the hyperbola is:
\[ (y + \sqrt{10})^2 - x^2 = 15 + 6\sqrt{10} \]

The final answer is:
\[
\boxed{(y + \sqrt{10})^2 - x^2 = 5 + 6\sqrt{10}}

Alright, so I need to find the equation of a hyperbola given its foci and vertices. The user mentioned the points (0, ±5) for the foci and (0, ±3) for the vertices. Hmm, okay.

First, let me recall what defines a hyperbola. A hyperbola is the set of all points where the absolute difference of the distances to two fixed points (the foci) is constant. But more formally, the standard equation of a hyperbola depends on whether it's opening vertically or horizontally.

Looking at the given points: both foci and vertices are along the y-axis because their x-coordinates are 0. That tells me this hyperbola opens up and down, so it's a vertical hyperbola.

For a standard vertical hyperbola centered at (h, k), the equation is:

\[
\frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Where:
- 'a' is the distance from the center to each vertex.
- 'c' is the distance from the center to each focus.
- The relationship between a, b, and c for hyperbolas is \(c^2 = a^2 + b^2\).

Given that both foci are at (0, ±5), the center of the hyperbola must be halfway between them. Since they're symmetric around the origin, the center is (0, 0). So h = 0 and k = 0.

The vertices are at (0, ±3), so a is the distance from the center to each vertex, which is 3 units. Therefore, a^2 = 9.

The foci are at (0, ±5), so c is 5, meaning c^2 = 25.

Now I can plug these into the relationship:

\(c^2 = a^2 + b^2\)

So,

25 = 9 + b^2

Subtracting 9 from both sides gives:

b^2 = 16

Therefore, b is 4.

Putting it all together into the standard equation:

\[
\frac{y^2}{9} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1
\]

I should double-check if everything makes sense. The hyperbola opens vertically because a and c are along the y-axis, which aligns with the given points. Also, since b^2 is positive, that's correct.

So yeah, that seems right. I think this is the equation they're looking for.

The hyperbola has its foci at (0, ±5) and vertices at (0, ±3), indicating it opens vertically. Using the standard form of a vertical hyperbola centered at the origin:

\[
\frac{y^2}{9} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the orientation:** Since both foci and vertices lie on the y-axis, the hyperbola opens vertically.

2. **Determine a and c:**
   - Vertices are at (0, ±3), so \(a = 3\) → \(a^2 = 9\).
   - Foci are at (0, ±5), so \(c = 5\) → \(c^2 = 25\).

3. **Find b using the relationship for hyperbolas (\(c^2 = a^2 + b^2\)):**
   \[
   25 = 9 + b^2 \Rightarrow b^2 = 16
   \]

4. **Plug values into the standard equation:**
   \[
   \frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1 \Rightarrow \frac{y^2}{9} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the hyperbola is \(\frac{y^2}{9} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1\).

Given the problem statement and potential formatting issues, we need to find the equation of a parabola. The key information provided includes the vertex at (0, 4) and an assumption about another point through which the parabola passes.

1. Recognize the ambiguity in the problem statement due to potential typos or formatting issues.
2. Assume the parabola passes through another standard point for simplicity.
3. Choose a common point such as (2, 0) for solving the equation of the parabola.
4. Use the vertex form of the parabola's equation: \( y = ax^2 + 4 \).
5. Substitute the assumed point (2, 0) into the equation to find the coefficient \( a \):
   \[
   0 = a(2)^2 + 4 \implies 0 = 4a + 4 \implies 4a = -4 \implies a = -1
   \]
6. The resulting equation of the parabola is:
   \[
   y = -x^2 + 4
   \]

Thus, the final answer is:

\[
\boxed{y = -x^2 + 4}
\]

To find the equation(s) of the parabola passing through the points (3,4) and (-1,2), we start with the general form y = ax² + bx + c. Substituting each point into this equation gives:

- **Focus**: The standard form of a right-opening parabola is \( y^2 = 4px \). Comparing with the given equation, we have \( 4p = 32 \), so \( p = 8 \). Therefore, the focus is at \( (8, 0) \).

- **Directrix**: For a right-opening parabola, the directrix is the line \( x = -p \). Substituting \( p = 8 \), the directrix is \( x = -8 \).

The equation of the parabola with a vertex at (2, 3) and a directrix at x = -1 is given by:

\[
(y - 3)^2 = 12(x - 2)
\]

Step-by-Step Explanation:
1. **Identify the direction**: Since the directrix is vertical (x = -1), the parabola opens horizontally.
2. **Determine p**:
   - The vertex form for a horizontal parabola opening right or left is \((y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h)\).
   - The distance from the vertex to the directrix is |h - (-1)|, which is 3 units.
   - Therefore, \(p = 3\) (positive because it opens towards increasing x-values).
3. **Formulate the equation**:
   - Substitute into \((y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h)\), giving \((y - 3)^2 = 12(x - 2)\).

Answer: \boxed{(y-3)^2=12(x-2)}

Alright, so I have to find the equation of a parabola that passes through three points: (2, 8), (-1, 3), and (0, 4). Hmm, okay. Let me think about how to approach this.

First, I remember that the general form of a quadratic equation is y = ax² + bx + c. Since we have three points, I can set up three equations with these coefficients as variables.

Let's start by plugging in each point into the equation. 

Starting with (0, 4): when x is 0, y should be 4. Plugging that into the general form gives me 4 = a*(0)² + b*0 + c, which simplifies to 4 = c. So I already know that c is 4.

Now, moving on to the second point (-1, 3). Plugging x = -1 and y = 3 into the equation: 3 = a*(-1)² + b*(-1) + c. Since we know c is 4, this becomes 3 = a*1 - b + 4. Simplifying that gives me a - b = 3 - 4, which is a - b = -1. So equation one from the second point: a - b = -1.

Next, using the third point (2, 8). Plugging x = 2 and y = 8 into the general form: 8 = a*(2)² + b*2 + c. Again, substituting c as 4, this becomes 8 = 4a + 2b + 4. Subtracting 4 from both sides gives me 4a + 2b = 4.

Now I have two equations:

1. a - b = -1
2. 4a + 2b = 4

I can solve these simultaneously to find the values of a and b. Let's use substitution or elimination. Maybe elimination is easier here.

From equation 1: a = b -1 (since a - b = -1, so a = b -1). 

Now plug this into equation 2:

4*(b - 1) + 2b = 4
Expanding that:
4b - 4 + 2b = 4
Combine like terms:
6b - 4 = 4
Add 4 to both sides:
6b = 8
Divide by 6:
b = 8/6, which simplifies to b = 4/3.

Now that I have b, substitute back into a = b -1:

a = (4/3) - 1 = 4/3 - 3/3 = 1/3.

So now we have a = 1/3, b = 4/3, and c = 4. 

Putting it all together, the equation of the parabola is y = (1/3)x² + (4/3)x + 4. To make it look neater, I can write it as y = (x² + 4x)/3 + 4 or even combine the constants into a single fraction if needed.

For (0,4): plugging x=0 gives y=0 + 0 + 4 = 4. Correct.

For (-1,3): y = (1/3)(1) + (4/3)(-1) + 4 = (1 -4)/3 +4 = (-3)/3 +4 = -1 +4 = 3. Correct.

For (2,8): y = (1/3)(4) + (4/3)(2) +4 = 4/3 + 8/3 +4 = 12/3 +4 = 4 +4=8. Correct.

Everything checks out!
</think>

After solving the system:
- a = 1/3
- b = 4/3
- c = 4

Thus, the equation is y = (1/3)x² + (4/3)x + 4.

The given problem involves finding the standard form of a parabola based on its definition and properties.

**Given:**
- The parabola has its vertex at (-4,1).
- The directrix is the line x = -6.

**Process:**

1. **Determine the Direction of Opening:**
   Since the directrix is vertical (x = -6) and the vertex is not on this line, the parabola opens to the right or left. The focus will be opposite the directrix relative to the vertex.

2. **Find the Focus:**
   The distance from the vertex to the directrix is 1 unit (from x=-4 to x=-6). Therefore, p = 1.
   Since it opens towards increasing x-values, the focus is at (-3,1).

3. **Write the Standard Form Equation:**
   For a parabola opening horizontally, standard form is:
   \[
   (y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h)
   \]
   Plugging in h=-4, k=1, and p=1:

\[
(y - 1)^2 = 4(1)(x + 4) \\
\Rightarrow (y - 1)^2 = 4(x + 4)
\]

**Final Answer:**
The standard form of the parabola is \(\boxed{(y - 1)^2 = 4(x + 4)}\).

The equation of a circle with center at \((h, k)\) and radius \(r\) is given by:

\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]

Given:
- Center: \((5, -2)\), so \(h = 5\) and \(k = -2\)
- Radius: \(5\), so \(r = 5\)

Substituting these values into the formula:

\[
(x - 5)^2 + (y - (-2))^2 = 5^2
\]
\[
(x - 5)^2 + (y + 2)^2 = 25
\]

**Final Answer:**
The equation of the circle is \(\boxed{(x-5)^2+(y+2)^2=25}\).

Okay, so I have this problem where I need to find the equation of a circle that has its diameter endpoints at (-2, 1) and (6, -3). Hmm, let's think about how to approach this. 

First off, I remember that the center of a circle is the midpoint between any two endpoints of its diameter. So, I guess I should find the midpoint of these two points first. The midpoint formula is ((x1 + x2)/2, (y1 + y2)/2), right? Let me plug in the values.

For the x-coordinates: (-2 + 6) divided by 2. That would be (4)/2 = 2. Okay, so the x-coordinate of the center is 2.

Now for the y-coordinates: (1 + (-3)) divided by 2. That's (-2)/2 = -1. So, the y-coordinate of the center is -1. Therefore, the center of the circle is at (2, -1). Got that part down.

Next up is finding the radius of the circle. I know the radius is half the diameter, so I need to calculate the distance between the two given points and then divide by 2. The distance formula between two points (x1, y1) and (x2, y2) is sqrt[(x2 - x1)^2 + (y2 - y1)^2]. Let's compute that.

The difference in x-coordinates is 6 - (-2), which is 8. Squared, that gives 64. The difference in y-coordinates is -3 - 1, which is -4. Squared, that gives 16. Adding those together: 64 + 16 = 80. Taking the square root of 80 simplifies to 4*sqrt(5). So, the diameter is 4√5, and half of that is 2√5. Therefore, the radius is 2√5.

Now that I have the center (h, k) as (2, -1) and the radius r as 2√5, I can plug these into the standard equation of a circle: (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2. Substituting the values gives:

(x - 2)^2 + (y - (-1))^2 = (2√5)^2.

Simplifying further, that becomes:

(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 4*5.

Which simplifies to:

(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 20.

Let me just double-check my steps to make sure I didn't make any mistakes. Calculated the midpoint correctly, used the distance formula properly, simplified everything right... Yeah, that seems correct.

The equation of the circle with diameter endpoints at (-2, 1) and (6, -3) is determined by finding its center as the midpoint between these points and calculating its radius from the distance between them.

**Step-by-step explanation:**

1. **Find the Midpoint (Center):**
   The midpoint M has coordinates:
   \[
   M_x = \frac{-2 + 6}{2} = 2, \quad M_y = \frac{1 + (-3)}{2} = -1
   \]
   So, the center is at (2, -1).

2. **Calculate the Radius:**
   The distance between the two points (diameter) is found using the distance formula:
   \[
   d = \sqrt{(6 - (-2))^2 + (-3 - 1)^2} = \sqrt{8^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{64 + 16} = \sqrt{80} = 4\sqrt{5}
   \]
   The radius is half the diameter:
   \[
   r = \frac{d}{2} = \frac{4\sqrt{5}}{2} = 2\sqrt{5}
   \]

3. **Write the Equation of the Circle:**
   Using the standard form with center (h, k) and radius r:
   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]
   Substituting the values:
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = (2\sqrt{5})^2
   \]
   Simplifying:
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 20
   \]

**Final Answer:**
The equation of the circle is \((x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 20\).

The given equation represents a circle with its center at \((-3, 2)\) and a radius of \(5\).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Start with the original equation:**
   \[
   X^2 + Y^2 + 6X - 4Y - 12 = 0
   \]

2. **Rearrange to group like terms and move constants to one side:**
   Move the constant term (-12) to the right:
   \[
   X^2 + 6X + Y^2 - 4Y = 12
   \]

3. **Complete the square for both \(X\) and \(Y\):**
   - For \(X^2 + 6X\):
     Add \((6/2)^2 = 9\) to complete the square: \( (X + 3)^2 = X^2 + 6X + 9\)
   - For \(Y^2 - 4Y\):
     Add \((-4/2)^2 = 4\) to complete the square: \( (Y - 2)^2 = Y^2 - 4Y + 4\)

4. **Add these squares to both sides of the equation:**
   \[
   X^2 + 6X + 9 + Y^2 - 4Y + 4 = 12 + 9 + 4
   \]
   Simplify:
   \[
   (X + 3)^2 + (Y - 2)^2 = 25
   \]

5. **Identify the center and radius:**
   The equation is now in standard form \((X - h)^2 + (Y - k)^2 = r^2\), so:
   - Center: \((h, k) = (-3, 2)\)
   - Radius: \(r = \sqrt{25} = 5\)

**Answer:**  
The center of the circle is at \((-3, 2)\) and its radius is \(5\).

The given equation x² + y² - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0 can be converted into the standard form of a circle by completing the squares for both x and y terms.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Start with the given equation:**
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0
   \]

2. **Rearrange the terms to group like variables:**
   \[
   (x^2 - 4x) + (y^2 + 2y) = 11
   \]

3. **Complete the square for x and y separately:**

   - For \( x^2 - 4x \):
     - Take half of the coefficient of x, which is \(-2\), square it to get 4.
     - Add and subtract this value:
       \[
       (x^2 - 4x + 4) = (x - 2)^2
       \]

   - For \( y^2 + 2y \):
     - Take half of the coefficient of y, which is \(1\), square it to get 1.
     - Add and subtract this value:
       \[
       (y^2 + 2y + 1) = (y + 1)^2
       \]

4. **Add these squares to both sides of the equation:**
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 11 + 4 + 1
   \]
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16
   \]

5. **Final standard form of the circle:**
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 4^2
   \]
   
**Answer:**  
\boxed{(x-2)^2 + (y+1)^2 = 16}

For a circle centered at (h, k) with radius r:

- If the circle touches the x-axis, the y-coordinate of the center equals the radius. So, if it's tangent to the x-axis, then k = r.

r = 4

To determine if the circle's equation is valid given that it touches the x-axis and has its center at \((3, 4)\), let's follow these steps:

1. **Understand the Conditions:**
   - The circle touches (is tangent to) the x-axis.
   - The center of the circle is at \((h, k) = (3, 4)\).

2. **Determine the Radius:**
   - When a circle touches the x-axis, its radius (\(r\)) is equal to its y-coordinate \(k\).
     \[
     r = |k| = 4
     \]

3. **Write the Standard Equation of the Circle:**
   - The standard equation for a circle with center \((h, k)\) and radius \(r\) is:
     \[
     (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
     \]
   - Substituting the known values (\(h = 3\), \(k = 4\), \(r = 4\)):
     \[
     (x - 3)^2 + (y - 4)^2 = 16
     \]

4. **Conclusion:**
   - The equation of the circle that touches the x-axis and has its center at \((3, 4)\) is:
     \[
     (x - 3)^2 + (y - 4)^2 = 16
     \]

**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{(x - 3)^2 + (y - 4)^2 = 16}

x² + 3x - 4 = 0

The quadratic formula is:

x = [-b ± √(b² - 4ac)] / (2a)

In this equation, a = 1, b = 3, and c = -4. Plugging those values in:

Discriminant D = 3² - 4*1*(-4) = 9 + 16 = 25

Since the discriminant is positive, there are two real solutions.

x = [-3 ± √25] / 2

x = (-3 ± 5)/2

First solution: x = (-3 + 5)/2 = 2/2 = 1
Second solution: x = (-3 - 5)/2 = -8/2 = -4

For x=-4:
y = -4 + 3 = -1

Checking (-4,-1):

In y = x + 3: -1 = -4 + 3 → -1=-1 ✔️

In the circle equation: (-4)² + (-1)^2 = 16 + 1 = 17 ✔️

Everything checks out, so these are indeed the correct intersection points.

Similarly, for y terms: Coefficient is 10/7. Half is 5/7, squared is 25/49.

So add these to both sides:

\(x^2 - (37/7)x + 1369/196 + y^2 + (10/7)y +25/49 = -13/7 + 1369/196 +25/49\)

Simplify the left side:

\((x - 37/(14))^2 + (y + 5/7)^2\)

Right side: Convert all to 196 denominator.

-13/7 = (-13 *28)/196= -364/196

1369/196 remains same, 25/49 = (25*4)/196=100/196

So total: (-364 +1369 +100)/196 = (1105)/196.

Therefore, the equation in center-radius form is:

\((x - 37/14)^2 + (y +5/7)^2 = 1105/196\)

Which can be written as:

\(\left(x - \frac{37}{14}\right)^2 + \left(y + \frac{5}{7}\right)^2 = \left(\sqrt{\frac{1105}{196}}\right)^2 = \left(\frac{\sqrt{1105}}{14}\right)^2\)

But the problem just asks for the equation of the circle, so both forms are correct. However, since it didn't specify, perhaps the standard form with fractions is acceptable.
</think>

The equation of the circle passing through the points (1, 1), (2, -3), and (5, -1) is found using the general equation \(x^2 + y^2 + Dx + Ey + F = 0\). Substituting each point into this equation yields a system of three linear equations in D, E, and F. Solving this system gives D = -37/7, E = 10/7, and F = 13/7. Thus, the equation is:

$$
x^2 + y^2 - \frac{37}{7}x + \frac{10}{7}y + \frac{13}{7} = 0
$$

Answer: \boxed{x^{2}+y^{2}-\dfrac{37}{7}x+\dfrac{10}{7}y+\dfrac{13}{7}=0}

The given equation of the circle can be rewritten in standard form by completing the square for both \( x \) and \( y \) terms:

1. Start with the original equation:
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 - 8x - 6y + 20 = 0
   \]
   
2. Group like terms:
   \[
   (x^2 - 8x) + (y^2 - 6y) = -20
   \]

3. Complete the square for \( x \):
   - Take half of \(-8\), which is \(-4\), and square it to get \(16\).
   - Add and subtract \(16\) within the equation:
     \[
     (x^2 - 8x + 16) - 16
     \]

4. Complete the square for \( y \):
   - Take half of \(-6\), which is \(-3\), and square it to get \(9\).
   - Add and subtract \(9\) within the equation:
     \[
     (y^2 - 6y + 9) - 9
     \]

5. Substitute back into the equation:
   \[
   (x - 4)^2 - 16 + (y - 3)^2 - 9 = -20
   \]
   
6. Combine constants on the left side:
   \[
   (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 - 25 = -20
   \]
   
7. Move constant to the right side by adding \(25\) to both sides:
   \[
   (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = 5
   \]

8. Now, the equation is in standard form, where the center of the circle is at \((4, 3)\) and the radius is \( \sqrt{5} \).

**Final Answer**
The circle has a radius of \( \sqrt{5} \) and its center is at (4, 3).

The given equation \(9x^2 + 4y^2 = 36\) can be converted into its standard conic section form by dividing each term by 36.

Step-by-step explanation:
1. Start with the original equation: \(9x^2 + 4y^2 = 36\).
2. Divide every term by 36 to normalize it: \(\frac{9x^2}{36} + \frac{4y^2}{36} = \frac{36}{36}\).
3. Simplify the fractions:
   - \(\frac{9x^2}{36} = \frac{x^2}{4}\)
   - \(\frac{4y^2}{36} = \frac{y^2}{9}\)
   - \(\frac{36}{36} = 1\)
4. Write the simplified equation: \(\frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1\).

This is now in the standard form of an ellipse, where \(a^2 = 4\) and \(b^2 = 9\), indicating that the major axis is along the y-axis.

Eccentricity (e) measures how "stretched" the ellipse is. The formula for eccentricity is e = c/a, where c is the distance from the center to each focus. But I need to find c first. From what I remember, in an ellipse, a² = b² + c². So rearranging that, c² = a² - b².

The given problem is translated and interpreted as finding the equation of an ellipse with foci at (40, 0) and (-40, 0). 

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Foci:** The foci are located at points (40, 0) and (-40, 0).

2. **Determine the Center:** The center of the ellipse is the midpoint between the two foci:
   - Midpoint formula: ((x₁ + x₂)/2, (y₁ + y₂)/2)
   - Calculation: ((-40 + 40)/2, (0 + 0)/2) = (0, 0)

3. **Calculate the Distance Between Foci:** The distance between the two foci is:
   - From (-40, 0) to (40, 0): 80 units

4. **Find 'c' (Distance from Center to Each Focus):** 
   - c = total distance / 2 = 80 / 2 = 40

5. **Determine the Length of the Major Axis:** The major axis length is given as 100, so:
   - Semi-major axis 'a' = 100 / 2 = 50

6. **Calculate 'b' (Semi-minor Axis):**
   - Using the relationship for ellipses: a² = b² + c²
   - Plug in known values: 50² = b² + 40² ⇒ 2500 = b² + 1600
   - Solve for 'b': b² = 2500 - 1600 = 900 ⇒ b = √900 = 30

7. **Write the Standard Equation of the Ellipse:**
   - Since the major axis is along the x-axis, the equation is:
     (x² / a²) + (y² / b²) = 1
   - Plugging in 'a' and 'b':
     x²/2500 + y²/900 = 1

**Final Answer:**

The standard form of the ellipse's equation is:
$$
\frac{x^2}{2500} + \frac{y^2}{900} = 1
$$

No such real hyperbola exists because for a hyperbola, the distance from the center to the foci (c) must be greater than the distance from the center to the vertices (a). Here, c = 5 and a = 7, which violates this condition. Therefore, there is no real hyperbola with these parameters.

$\boxed{\text{No such hyperbola exists}}$

Given the problem of finding a hyperbola that passes through two points, we need to set up a system of equations. The standard form of a hyperbola with a vertical transverse axis is:

\[
\frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

For two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\), we substitute these coordinates into the hyperbola equation to form a system of equations. This results in the following system:

\[
\frac{(y_1 - k)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(x_1 - h)^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

and

\[
\frac{(y_2 - k)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(x_2 - h)^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Thus, the system of equations for finding the hyperbola parameters is:

\[
\boxed{\frac{(y_1 - k)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(x_1 - h)^2}{b^2} = 1}
\]

and

\[
\boxed{\frac{(y_2 - k)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(x_2 - h)^2}{b^2} = 1}
\]

The given equation \( \frac{X^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \) represents an ellipse. The standard form of an ellipse is \( \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1 \), where a and b are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes, respectively.

- \( b^2 = 9 \) → \( b = 3 \)

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Here, 'a' is the distance from the center to each vertex, and 'c' is the distance from the center to each focus. The relationship between a, b, and c for hyperbolas is given by:

\[
c^2 = a^2 + b^2
\]

So, let's plug in the values we have.

The vertices are at (±4, 0), so 'a' must be 4 because that's the distance from the center (which is at the origin since there's no shift) to each vertex. So,

\[
a = 4 \implies a^2 = 16
\]

Similarly, the foci are at (±6, 0), so 'c' must be 6 because that's the distance from the center to each focus. Thus,

\[
c = 6 \implies c^2 = 36
\]

Now, using the relationship between a, b, and c:

\[
c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \\
36 = 16 + b^2 \\
b^2 = 36 - 16 \\
b^2 = 20 \\
\]

So now we can write the equation of the hyperbola by substituting back into the standard form:

\[
\frac{x^2}{16} - \frac{y^2}{20} = 1
\]

I remember that hyperbolas can be oriented either horizontally or vertically. The general form for a horizontally oriented hyperbola is (x - h)²/a² - (y - k)²/b² = 1, where (h,k) is the center. Now, for hyperbolas, I know that the asymptotes are the lines that the hyperbola approaches but never touches. The equations of these asymptotes depend on whether the hyperbola opens horizontally or vertically. Since our hyperbola opens horizontally (because the positive term is with x²), the slopes of the asymptotes will be ±(b/a).

Looking at the given equation, it's x²/4 - y²/9 = 1, so the positive term is with x², which means this is a horizontally oriented hyperbola. That makes sense because for horizontal hyperbolas, the standard form is (x-h)²/a² - (y-k)²/b² = 1, where (h,k) is the center.

y = (3/2)x and y = -(3/2)x

To find the hyperbola equation centered at (0, √10) and passing through the point (2, 3), we start by considering the general form of a hyperbola centered at (h, k). For a vertical hyperbola centered at (0, √10), the standard form is:

\[
\frac{(y - \sqrt{10})^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Substituting the point (2, 3) into this equation, we get:

\[
\frac{(3 - \sqrt{10})^2}{a^2} - \frac{4}{b^2} = 1
\]

Calculating \((3 - \sqrt{10})^2\):

\[
(3 - \sqrt{10})^2 = 9 - 6\sqrt{10} + 10 = 19 - 6\sqrt{10}
\]

Thus, the equation becomes:

\[
\frac{19 - 6\sqrt{10}}{a^2} - \frac{4}{b^2} = 1
\]

However, without additional information such as another point on the hyperbola or an asymptote, we cannot uniquely determine the values of \(a\) and \(b\). Therefore, it is not possible to find a unique hyperbola equation with the given information.

Okay, so I need to find the equation of a hyperbola given its foci and vertices. The points provided are (0, ±5) for the foci and (0, ±3) for the vertices. Hmm, let's break this down step by step.

First, I remember that hyperbolas can be oriented either vertically or horizontally depending on whether their transverse axis is along the x-axis or y-axis. In this case, both the foci and vertices have the same x-coordinate (0), so it looks like the transverse axis is vertical. That means the hyperbola opens upwards and downwards.

For a standard hyperbola with a vertical transverse axis, the equation is of the form:

(y²/a²) - (x²/b²) = 1

Here, 'a' represents the distance from the center to each vertex along the y-axis, and 'c' is the distance from the center to each focus. The relationship between a, b, and c for hyperbolas is given by:

c² = a² + b²

I know that the vertices are at (0, ±3), so 'a' must be 3 because it's the distance from the center (which I assume is at the origin since no other information is given) to each vertex. Similarly, the foci are at (0, ±5), so 'c' is 5.

Plugging these values into the relationship equation:

c² = a² + b²  
5² = 3² + b²  
25 = 9 + b²  
b² = 25 - 9  
b² = 16

So, b is 4. Now that I have a² and b², I can plug these back into the standard equation.

Therefore, the equation becomes:

(y²/9) - (x²/16) = 1

Let me just double-check to make sure everything makes sense. The center is at (0,0), which is correct because both vertices and foci are symmetric around the origin. The values of a, b, c all follow the relationship correctly: 3² + 4² = 5² → 9 + 16 =25→25=25.

I think that's it. The equation should be (y²/9) - (x²/16)=1.

The hyperbola has its center at the origin, with a vertical transverse axis because both foci and vertices are on the y-axis. Using the standard form of a vertically oriented hyperbola:

(y²/a²) - (x²/b²) = 1

Given:
- Vertices at (0, ±3): So, a = 3.
- Foci at (0, ±5): So, c = 5.

Using the relationship c² = a² + b²:
25 = 9 + b²  
b² = 16  
b = 4

Thus, the equation of the hyperbola is:

(y²/9) - (x²/16) = 1
